```python
import pandas as pd
from datetime import datetime
from collections import Counter

def analyze_user_behavior(data):
    """
    Analyzes user behavior on a website using a dataset of user interactions.

    Parameters:
    data (list of dict): A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents an interaction
                         with keys 'user_id', 'timestamp', 'event_type', and 'page'.

    Returns:
    tuple: A tuple containing:
           1. An integer representing the number of unique users.
           2. A float representing the average time spent on the website per user in minutes.
           3. A string representing the most common event type.
           4. A list of strings representing the top 5 most visited pages.
    """
    df = pd.DataFrame(data)
    df['timestamp'] = pd.to_datetime(df['timestamp'])
    df.sort_values(by=['user_id', 'timestamp'], inplace=True)
    df['time_diff'] = df.groupby('user_id')['timestamp'].diff().fillna(pd.Timedelta(0))
    df['time_diff_minutes'] = df['time_diff'].dt.total_seconds() / 60
    unique_users = df['user_id'].nunique()
    avg_time_spent = df.groupby('user_id')['time_diff_minutes'].sum().mean()
    most_common_event = df['event_type'].mode()[0]
    page_visits = df[df['event_type'] == 'page_view']['page'].value_counts().head(5)
    top_5_visited_pages = page_visits.index.tolist()
    return unique_users, avg_time_spent, most_common_event, top_5_visited_pages
```<#sep#>Implement a Python class `DatabaseConnection` that manages basic operations on an SQLite database, including connecting to the database, executing queries, and closing the connection. The class should handle exceptions gracefully. Your implementation should include methods for creating a connection, executing a query, fetching all rows from a query, fetching a single row from a query, and closing the connection.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
db = DatabaseConnection("example.db")
db.create_connection()
db.execute_query("CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT)")
db.execute_query("INSERT INTO users (name) VALUES (?)", ("Alice",))
db.execute_query("INSERT INTO users (name) VALUES (?)", ("Bob",))
print(db.fetch_all("SELECT * FROM users"))
print(db.fetch_one("SELECT name FROM users WHERE id = ?", (1,)))
db.close_connection()
```

**Expected Output:**
```
Successfully connected to SQLite database: example.db
Query successfully executed
Query successfully executed
Query successfully executed
[(1, 'Alice'), (2, 'Bob')]
('Alice',)
Database connection closed
```

```python
import sqlite3
from sqlite3 import Error

class DatabaseConnection:
    def __init__(self, db_file):
        """
        Initialize the DatabaseConnection with the path to the database file.
        
        :param db_file: Path to the SQLite database file.
        """
        self.connection = None
        self.db_file = db_file

    def create_connection(self):
        """
        Create a database connection to the SQLite database.
        """
        try:
            self.connection = sqlite3.connect(self.db_file)
            print(f"Successfully connected to SQLite database: {self.db_file}")
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while connecting to the database: {e}")

    def execute_query(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Execute a single SQL query.
        
        :param query: SQL query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the query.
        :return: Cursor object if successful, None otherwise.
        """
        if self.connection is None:
            print("No database connection. Please call create_connection() first.")
            return None
        cursor = self.connection.cursor()
        try:
            if params:
                cursor.execute(query, params)
            else:
                cursor.execute(query)
            self.connection.commit()
            print("Query successfully executed")
            return cursor
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            cursor.close()

    def fetch_all(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Fetch all rows from the database using a SELECT query.
        
        :param query: SQL SELECT query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the query.
        :return: List of rows if successful, None otherwise.
        """
        cursor = self.execute_query(query, params)
        try:
            if cursor:
                rows = cursor.fetchall()
                return rows
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while fetching data: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            cursor.close()

    def fetch_one(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Fetch a single row from the database using a SELECT query.
        
        :param query: SQL SELECT query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the query.
        :return: Single row if successful, None otherwise.
        """
        cursor = self.execute_query(query, params)
        try:
            if cursor:
                row = cursor.fetchone()
                return row
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while fetching data: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            cursor.close()

    def close_connection(self):
        """
        Close the database connection.
        """
        try:
            if self.connection:
                self.connection.close()
                print("Database connection closed")
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while closing the connection: {e}")
```<#sep#>You are tasked with creating a function `generate_sudoku` that generates a valid 9x9 Sudoku puzzle with a unique solution. The function should return the puzzle as a 2D list, where `0` represents an empty cell. The generated puzzle should have a unique solution and should be solvable.

**Sample Input:**
None

**Sample Output:**
```
[
 [5, 3, 0, 0, 7, 0, 0, 0, 0],
 [6, 0, 0, 1, 9, 5, 0, 0, 0],
 [0, 9, 8, 0, 0, 0, 0, 6, 0],
 [8, 0, 0, 0, 6, 0, 0, 0, 3],
 [4, 0, 0, 8, 0, 3, 0, 0, 1],
 [7, 0, 0, 0, 2, 0, 0, 0, 6],
 [0, 6, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2, 8, 0],
 [0, 0, 0, 4, 1, 9, 0, 0, 5],
 [0, 0, 0, 0, 8, 0, 0, 7, 9]
]
```

```python
import random

def is_valid(board, row, col, num):
    """
    Check if placing `num` at position (row, col) in the board is valid.
    """
    for i in range(9):
        if board[row][i] == num or board[i][col] == num:
            return False

    start_row, start_col = 3 * (row // 3), 3 * (col // 3)
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            if board[i + start_row][j + start_col] == num:
                return False
    return True

def solve_sudoku(board):
    """
    Solve the Sudoku puzzle using backtracking.
    """
    empty = find_empty_location(board)
    if not empty:
        return True  # Puzzle solved
    row, col = empty

    for num in range(1, 10):
        if is_valid(board, row, col, num):
            board[row][col] = num
            if solve_sudoku(board):
                return True
            board[row][col] = 0  # Backtrack
    return False

def find_empty_location(board):
    """
    Find an empty location (represented by 0) in the board.
    """
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if board[i][j] == 0:
                return (i, j)
    return None

def count_solutions(board, row_col):
    """
    Count the number of solutions for the Sudoku puzzle starting from position (row, col).
    """
    row, col = row_col
    num_solutions = 0
    for num in range(1, 10):
        if is_valid(board, row, col, num):
            board[row][col] = num
            if not find_empty_location(board):
                num_solutions += 1
            else:
                num_solutions += count_solutions(board, find_empty_location(board))
            board[row][col] = 0  # Backtrack
    return num_solutions

def generate_sudoku():
    """
    Generate a valid 9x9 Sudoku puzzle with a unique solution.
    """
    while True:
        # Create a solved Sudoku
        board = [[0] * 9 for _ in range(9)]
        numbers = list(range(1, 10))
        
        for i in range(9):
            random.shuffle(numbers)
            for j in range(9):
                if is_valid(board, i, j, numbers[j]):
                    board[i][j] = numbers[j]
                    break
            else:
                break  # If no valid number is found, restart
        else:
            break  # If the loop completes, the board is fully filled
        
        # Create the puzzle by removing numbers
        attempts = 5
        while attempts > 0:
            row, col = random.randint(0, 8), random.randint(0, 8)
            while board[row][col] == 0:
                row, col = random.randint(0, 8), random.randint(0, 8)
            num = board[row][col]
            board[row][col] = 0

            # Make a copy of the board to count the number of solutions
            temp_board = [row[:] for row in board]

            if find_empty_location(temp_board):
                num_solutions = count_solutions(temp_board, (row, col))
                if num_solutions != 1:
                    board[row][col] = num
                    attempts -= 1
        return board
```<#sep#>You are tasked with generating a random maze of a specified size using the Depth-First Search (DFS) algorithm. The maze should be represented as a 2D grid where `0` indicates a path and `1` indicates a wall. The maze should have a border of walls and should be fully connected, meaning there is a path from any point in the maze to any other point.

**Input:**
- Two integers `W` and `H` (1 ≤ W, H ≤ 20), representing the width and height of the maze, respectively.

**Output:**
- A 2D grid of size `(2*H + 1) x (2*W + 1)` where each cell is either `0` (path) or `1` (wall).

**Sample Input:**
```
3 3
```

**Sample Output:**
```
#########
#       #
# ### ###
# #   # #
# ### ###
#       #
#########
```

```python
import random

def generate_maze(width, height):
    """
    Generates a random maze of specified width and height using the Depth-First Search (DFS) algorithm.
    
    :param width: The width of the maze (number of cells).
    :param height: The height of the maze (number of cells).
    :return: A 2D list representing the maze, where 0 indicates a path and 1 indicates a wall.
    """
    # Initialize the maze with walls
    maze = [[1 for _ in range(width * 2 + 1)] for _ in range(height * 2 + 1)]
    
    # Directions for moving in the maze: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(0, -2), (0, 2), (-2, 0), (2, 0)]
    
    def is_valid(x, y):
        """
        Checks if the cell (x, y) is within the maze bounds and is a wall.
        
        :param x: The x-coordinate of the cell.
        :param y: The y-coordinate of the cell.
        :return: True if the cell is valid, False otherwise.
        """
        return 0 < x < height * 2 and 0 < y < width * 2 and maze[x][y] == 1
    
    def dfs(x, y):
        """
        Performs a depth-first search to carve out the maze starting from cell (x, y).
        
        :param x: The x-coordinate of the starting cell.
        :param y: The y-coordinate of the starting cell.
        """
        # Mark the current cell as a path
        maze[x][y] = 0
        
        # Shuffle the directions to ensure randomness
        random.shuffle(directions)
        
        for dx, dy in directions:
            nx, ny = x + dx, y + dy
            # Check if the next cell is valid and not visited
            if is_valid(nx, ny):
                # Mark the wall between the current cell and the next cell as a path
                maze[x + dx // 2][y + dy // 2] = 0
                # Visit the next cell
                dfs(nx, ny)
    
    # Start DFS from the top-left corner of the maze
    dfs(1, 1)
    
    return maze

def print_maze(maze):
    """
    Prints the maze in a human-readable format.
    
    :param maze: A 2D list representing the maze.
    """
    for row in maze:
        print(''.join(['#' if cell == 1 else ' ' for cell in row]))

# Example usage
maze_width = 3
maze_height = 3
maze = generate_maze(maze_width, maze_height)
print_maze(maze)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a caching mechanism for a function `fetch_user_data` that retrieves user data from a remote server. The caching mechanism should store the results of recent calls to the function and return the cached result if the same input is provided again. The cache should have a maximum capacity and should follow the Least Recently Used (LRU) eviction policy.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `C` (1 ≤ C ≤ 100), the capacity of the cache.
- The second line contains an integer `N` (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000), the number of user data requests.
- The next `N` lines each contain an integer `user_id` (1 ≤ user_id ≤ 1000), representing the user data request.

**Output:**
- For each request, print "Fetching data for user X" if the data is not in the cache, otherwise print "Returning cached data for user X".

**Sample Input:**
```
3
6
1
2
3
1
4
2
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Fetching data for user 1
Fetching data for user 2
Fetching data for user 3
Returning cached data for user 1
Fetching data for user 4
Fetching data for user 2
```

```python
from collections import OrderedDict

class LRUCache:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        """
        Initialize the LRU Cache with a given capacity.
        """
        self.cache = OrderedDict()
        self.capacity = capacity

    def get(self, key):
        """
        Retrieve item from cache and mark it as recently used.
        """
        if key in self.cache:
            self.cache.move_to_end(key)
            return self.cache[key]
        return None

    def put(self, key, value):
        """
        Insert item into cache, evicting the least recently used item if necessary.
        """
        if key in self.cache:
            self.cache.move_to_end(key)
        self.cache[key] = value
        if len(self.cache) > self.capacity:
            self.cache.popitem(last=False)

def fetch_user_data(user_id):
    """
    Simulate fetching user data from a remote server.
    """
    print(f"Fetching data for user {user_id}")
    return {"user_id": user_id, "data": "simulated user data"}

def cached_fetch_user_data(cache, user_id):
    """
    Fetch user data using the cache mechanism.
    """
    data = cache.get(user_id)
    if data is None:
        data = fetch_user_data(user_id)
        cache.put(user_id, data)
    else:
        print(f"Returning cached data for user {user_id}")
    return data

# Usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    C = int(data[0])
    N = int(data[1])
    requests = list(map(int, data[2:]))

    cache = LRUCache(C)
    for user_id in requests:
        cached_fetch_user_data(cache, user_id)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Matrix` class that supports basic matrix operations. Your class should include the following functionalities:

- **Initialization**: Create a matrix with specified dimensions. Optionally, initialize with given data.
- **String Representation**: Provide a string representation of the matrix.
- **Addition**: Add two matrices of the same dimensions.
- **Multiplication**: Multiply two matrices, ensuring the number of columns in the first matrix matches the number of rows in the second matrix.
- **Transpose**: Return the transpose of the matrix.
- **Determinant**: Compute the determinant of the matrix, valid only for square matrices.

Implement the `Matrix` class with the methods described above. Do not use any external libraries.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
# Initialization and String Representation
matrix1 = Matrix(2, 2, [[1, 2], [3, 4]])
print(matrix1)  # Output: 1 2\n3 4

# Addition
matrix2 = Matrix(2, 2, [[5, 6], [7, 8]])
matrix3 = matrix1 + matrix2
print(matrix3)  # Output: 6 8\n10 12

# Multiplication
matrix4 = Matrix(2, 2, [[2, 0], [1, 2]])
matrix5 = matrix1 * matrix4
print(matrix5)  # Output: 4 4\n8 14

# Transpose
matrix6 = matrix1.transpose()
print(matrix6)  # Output: 1 3\n2 4

# Determinant
det = matrix1.determinant()
print(det)  # Output: -2
```

```python
class Matrix:
    def __init__(self, rows, cols, data=None):
        """
        Initializes a matrix with given rows and columns.
        If data is provided, initializes the matrix with the given data.
        Otherwise, initializes a zero matrix.
        """
        if data:
            self.data = data
        else:
            self.data = [[0 for _ in range(cols)] for _ in range(rows)]
        self.rows = rows
        self.cols = cols

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the matrix.
        """
        return '\n'.join([' '.join(map(str, row)) for row in self.data])

    def __add__(self, other):
        """
        Adds two matrices of the same dimensions.
        Raises ValueError if dimensions do not match.
        """
        if self.rows != other.rows or self.cols != other.cols:
            raise ValueError("Matrices dimensions must match for addition")
        result = [[self.data[i][j] + other.data[i][j] for j in range(self.cols)] for i in range(self.rows)]
        return Matrix(self.rows, self.cols, result)

    def __mul__(self, other):
        """
        Multiplies two matrices.
        Raises ValueError if the number of columns in the first matrix
        does not match the number of rows in the second matrix.
        """
        if self.cols != other.rows:
            raise ValueError("Number of columns in the first matrix must be equal to the number of rows in the second matrix")
        result = [[sum(self.data[i][k] * other.data[k][j] for k in range(self.cols)) for j in range(other.cols)] for i in range(self.rows)]
        return Matrix(self.rows, other.cols, result)

    def transpose(self):
        """
        Returns the transpose of the matrix.
        """
        result = [[self.data[j][i] for j in range(self.rows)] for i in range(self.cols)]
        return Matrix(self.cols, self.rows, result)

    def minor(self, i, j):
        """
        Returns the minor of the matrix after removing the i-th row and j-th column.
        """
        return [row[:j] + row[j+1:] for row in (self.data[:i] + self.data[i+1:])]

    def determinant(self):
        """
        Computes the determinant of the matrix.
        Raises ValueError if the matrix is not square.
        """
        if self.rows != self.cols:
            raise ValueError("Determinant is only defined for square matrices")
        if self.rows == 1:
            return self.data[0][0]
        if self.rows == 2:
            return (self.data[0][0] * self.data[1][1]) - (self.data[0][1] * self.data[1][0])
        det = 0
        for c in range(self.cols):
            det += ((-1)**c) * self.data[0][c] * Matrix(self.minor(0, c)).determinant()
        return det
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a class hierarchy for vehicles. The base class `Car` should have the following attributes and methods:
- `make`: a string representing the make of the car.
- `model`: a string representing the model of the car.
- `year`: an integer representing the year the car was manufactured.
- `__init__(self, make, model, year)`: the constructor that initializes the car's attributes.

Create a subclass `ElectricCar` that inherits from `Car` and includes an additional attribute `battery_capacity` (an integer representing the battery capacity in kWh). The `ElectricCar` class should override the `display_info(self)` method to return a string with the car's information including the battery capacity in the format "Make: [make], Model: [model], Year: [year], Battery Capacity: [battery_capacity] kWh".

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance.

        :param make: The make of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year the car was manufactured.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Returns a string with the car's information.

        :return: A string in the format "Make: [make], Model: [model], Year: [year]".
        """
        return f"Make: {self.make}, Model: {self.model}, Year: {self.year}"


class ElectricCar(Car):
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, battery_capacity):
        """
        Initializes a new ElectricCar instance.

        :param make: The make of the electric car.
        :param model: The model of the electric car.
        :param year: The year the electric car was manufactured.
        :param battery_capacity: The battery capacity of the electric car in kWh.
        """
        super().__init__(make, model, year)
        self.battery_capacity = battery_capacity

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Returns a string with the electric car's information including battery capacity.

        :return: A string in the format "Make: [make], Model: [model], Year: [year], Battery Capacity: [battery_capacity] kWh".
        """
        return f"{super().display_info()}, Battery Capacity: {self.battery_capacity} kWh"
```<#sep#>You are given a list of strings `words`. For each string in the list, determine the longest substring that is a palindrome. A palindrome is a string that reads the same backward as forward. If there are multiple longest palindromic substrings of the same length, return any one of them. Your task is to return a list of tuples, where each tuple contains the original string and its longest palindromic substring.

**Input:**
- A list of strings `words` (1 ≤ len(words) ≤ 100), where each string consists of lowercase English letters and has a length between 1 and 1000.

**Output:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains the original string and its longest palindromic substring.

**Sample Input:**
```python
['racecar', 'banana', 'civic']
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[('racecar', 'racecar'), ('banana', 'anana'), ('civic', 'civic')]
```

```python
def longest_palindrome_substring(words):
    """
    Finds the longest palindromic substring for each string in the list.

    :param words: List of strings to process.
    :return: List of tuples, each containing the original string and its longest palindromic substring.
    """
    def expand_around_center(s, left, right):
        """
        Expands around the center to find the longest palindrome centered at left and right.

        :param s: The string to search within.
        :param left: The left index of the center.
        :param right: The right index of the center.
        :return: The longest palindromic substring centered at left and right.
        """
        while left >= 0 and right < len(s) and s[left] == s[right]:
            left -= 1
            right += 1
        return s[left + 1:right]
    
    def find_longest_palindrome(s):
        """
        Finds the longest palindromic substring in the given string.

        :param s: The string to search within.
        :return: The longest palindromic substring.
        """
        longest = ""
        for i in range(len(s)):
            # Odd length palindromes
            palindrome1 = expand_around_center(s, i, i)
            # Even length palindromes
            palindrome2 = expand_around_center(s, i, i + 1)
            
            # Update longest palindrome found
            if len(palindrome1) > len(longest):
                longest = palindrome1
            if len(palindrome2) > len(longest):
                longest = palindrome2
        return longest
    
    return [(word, find_longest_palindrome(word)) for word in words]

# Example usage
words = ['racecar', 'banana', 'civic']
print(longest_palindrome_substring(words))  # Output: [('racecar', 'racecar'), ('banana', 'anana'), ('civic', 'civic')]
```<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a program to generate a library report based on a provided dataset. The dataset is stored in a JSON file named `library_data.json` and contains a list of dictionaries, each representing a book with the following attributes:
- `title`: The title of the book.
- `author`: The author of the book.
- `checked_out`: A boolean indicating whether the book is currently checked out.
- `checkout_date`: The date the book was checked out, formatted as `YYYY-MM-DD` (only present if the book is checked out).

Your program should output a report that includes:
- The total number of books in the library.
- The number of books currently checked out.
- The number of books available for checkout.
- The average duration (in days) that books are checked out.

**Input:**
- A JSON file named `library_data.json` in the same directory as the program.

**Output:**
- A formatted report printed to the console.

**Sample Input (`library_data.json`):**
```json
[
    {"title": "Book A", "author": "Author X", "checked_out": true, "checkout_date": "2023-01-01"},
    {"title": "Book B", "author": "Author Y", "checked_out": false},
    {"title": "Book C", "author": "Author Z", "checked_out": true, "checkout_date": "2023-02-01"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Library Report
--------------
Total number of books: 3
Number of books checked out: 2
Number of books available: 1
Average checkout duration: 31 days
```

```python
import json
from datetime import datetime, timedelta

def generate_library_report(file_path):
    """
    Generates a library report based on the data in the specified JSON file.
    
    Parameters:
    file_path (str): The path to the JSON file containing library data.
    
    Returns:
    None: The function prints the report to the console.
    """
    with open(file_path, 'r') as file:
        books = json.load(file)
    
    total_books = len(books)
    checked_out_books = 0
    available_books = 0
    total_checkout_duration = timedelta()
    current_date = datetime.now()
    
    for book in books:
        if book['checked_out']:
            checked_out_books += 1
            checkout_date = datetime.strptime(book['checkout_date'], '%Y-%m-%d')
            checkout_duration = current_date - checkout_date
            total_checkout_duration += checkout_duration
        else:
            available_books += 1
    
    average_checkout_duration = total_checkout_duration / checked_out_books if checked_out_books else timedelta()
    
    print("Library Report")
    print("--------------")
    print(f"Total number of books: {total_books}")
    print(f"Number of books checked out: {checked_out_books}")
    print(f"Number of books available: {available_books}")
    print(f"Average checkout duration: {average_checkout_duration.days} days")

generate_library_report('library_data.json')
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that simulates basic banking operations. The class should support depositing money, withdrawing money, and checking the current balance. The constructor should allow initializing the account with an optional starting balance. Implement error handling for invalid operations, such as attempting to withdraw more than the current balance or depositing a negative amount. Your implementation should include docstrings for the class and its methods.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- **Input:** 
  ```python
  account = BankAccount(100.0)
  account.deposit(50.0)
  account.withdraw(30.0)
  print(account.display_balance())
  ```
- **Output:**
  ```
  120.0
  ```

```python
class BankAccount:
    """
    A simple representation of a bank account.

    Attributes:
    ----------
    balance : float
        The current balance of the account.

    Methods
    -------
    deposit(amount):
        Deposits a specified amount into the account.
    withdraw(amount):
        Withdraws a specified amount from the account, if sufficient funds are available.
    display_balance():
        Returns the current balance of the account.
    """

    def __init__(self, starting_balance=0.0):
        """
        Initializes the bank account with an optional starting balance.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        starting_balance : float, optional
            The starting balance of the account (default is 0.0).
        """
        self.balance = starting_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposits a specified amount into the account.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        amount : float
            The amount to deposit into the account.

        Raises:
        ------
        ValueError
            If the amount to deposit is negative.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraws a specified amount from the account, if sufficient funds are available.

        Parameters:
        ----------
        amount : float
            The amount to withdraw from the account.

        Raises:
        ------
        ValueError
            If the amount to withdraw is negative or greater than the current balance.
        """
        if amount < 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount

    def display_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.

        Returns:
        -------
        float
            The current balance of the account.
        """
        return self.balance
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `FileAnalyzer` that processes a text file to perform several analyses. Your class should be able to handle the following tasks:

1. Count the total number of lines in the file.
2. Count the total number of words in the file.
3. Count the total number of characters in the file.
4. Identify and return the most frequently occurring word in the file.
5. Identify and return the least frequently occurring word in the file.

The class should have the following methods:

- `__init__(self, filename)`: Initializes the class with the filename of the text file to be analyzed.
- `count_lines(self)`: Returns the total number of lines in the file.
- `count_words(self)`: Returns the total number of words in the file.
- `count_characters(self)`: Returns the total number of characters in the file.
- `most_frequent_word(self)`: Returns the most frequently occurring word in the file.
- `least_frequent_word(self)`: Returns the least frequently occurring word in the file.

**Sample Input File (sample.txt):**
```
Hello world
Hello everyone
This is a test
```

**Sample Output:**
- `count_lines()`: 3
- `count_words()`: 8
- `count_characters()`: 44
- `most_frequent_word()`: "hello"
- `least_frequent_word()`: "everyone", "is", "a", "test" (any one of these)

```python
import re
from collections import Counter

class FileAnalyzer:
    def __init__(self, filename):
        """
        Initializes the FileAnalyzer with the given filename.
        
        :param filename: str, the path to the text file to be analyzed.
        """
        self.filename = filename
        self.content = None
        self.words = None
        self.read_file()

    def read_file(self):
        """
        Reads the content of the file and extracts words.
        """
        with open(self.filename, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            self.content = file.read()
            self.words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', self.content.lower())

    def count_lines(self):
        """
        Counts the total number of lines in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of lines.
        """
        return len(self.content.splitlines())

    def count_words(self):
        """
        Counts the total number of words in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of words.
        """
        return len(self.words)

    def count_characters(self):
        """
        Counts the total number of characters in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of characters.
        """
        return len(self.content)

    def most_frequent_word(self):
        """
        Identifies and returns the most frequently occurring word in the file.
        
        :return: str, the most frequent word.
        """
        if not self.words:
            return None
        word_counts = Counter(self.words)
        most_common_word, _ = word_counts.most_common(1)[0]
        return most_common_word

    def least_frequent_word(self):
        """
        Identifies and returns the least frequently occurring word in the file.
        
        :return: str, the least frequent word.
        """
        if not self.words:
            return None
        word_counts = Counter(self.words)
        least_common_word, _ = word_counts.most_common()[-1]
        return least_common_word
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The class should encapsulate the properties of a book and provide methods to manage its availability. The `Book` class should have the following attributes:

- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN number of the book.
- `year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
- `available_copies`: An integer representing the number of copies currently available for checkout.
- `total_copies`: An integer representing the total number of copies in the library.

The class should also provide the following methods:

- `check_out`: Decrease the number of available copies by 1 if there are any available. If no copies are available, print a message stating that the book is currently not available.
- `return_book`: Increase the number of available copies by 1, ensuring it does not exceed the total number of copies.
- `get_details`: Return a string with the book's details in the format: "{title} by {author} (ISBN: {isbn}, Year: {year}) - {available_copies}/{total_copies} copies available".

Ensure that `available_copies` and `total_copies` are non-negative and that `available_copies` does not exceed `total_copies`.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949, 3, 5)
print(book.get_details())  # Output: "1984 by George Orwell (ISBN: 9780451524935, Year: 1949) - 3/5 copies available"
book.check_out()
print(book.get_details())  # Output: "1984 by George Orwell (ISBN: 9780451524935, Year: 1949) - 2/5 copies available"
book.return_book()
print(book.get_details())  # Output: "1984 by George Orwell (ISBN: 9780451524935, Year: 1949) - 3/5 copies available"
book.check_out()
book.check_out()
book.check_out()
book.check_out()  # Output: "The book '1984' is currently not available."
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, year, available_copies, total_copies):
        """
        Initialize a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN number of the book.
        :param year: The year the book was published.
        :param available_copies: The number of copies currently available.
        :param total_copies: The total number of copies in the library.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.year = year
        self.available_copies = max(0, min(available_copies, total_copies))
        self.total_copies = max(0, total_copies)

    def check_out(self):
        """
        Decrease the number of available copies by 1 if available.
        Print a message if no copies are available.
        """
        if self.available_copies > 0:
            self.available_copies -= 1
        else:
            print(f"The book '{self.title}' is currently not available.")

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Increase the number of available copies by 1, ensuring it does not exceed total copies.
        """
        if self.available_copies < self.total_copies:
            self.available_copies += 1

    def get_details(self):
        """
        Return a string with the book's details.

        :return: A formatted string with the book's details.
        """
        return (f"{self.title} by {self.author} (ISBN: {self.isbn}, Year: {self.year})"
                f" - {self.available_copies}/{self.total_copies} copies available")
```<#sep#>You are tasked with processing user interaction data from a JSON file named `user_data.json` for a web analytics project. The JSON file contains nested objects and arrays representing various user actions. Your goal is to write a Python script that reads this JSON file, flattens its nested structure, and writes the flattened data to a new JSON file named `flattened_data.json`. Your script must handle potential errors such as missing files or invalid JSON format.

**Sample Input:**
```json
{
    "user": {
        "id": 123,
        "name": "John Doe",
        "actions": [
            {"type": "click", "element": "button"},
            {"type": "hover", "element": "menu"}
        ]
    }
}
```

**Sample Output:**
```json
{
    "user_id": 123,
    "user_name": "John Doe",
    "user_actions_0_type": "click",
    "user_actions_0_element": "button",
    "user_actions_1_type": "hover",
    "user_actions_1_element": "menu"
}
```

```python
import json
import os

def flatten_json(y):
    """Flatten a nested JSON dictionary into a single-level dictionary."""
    out = {}

    def flatten(x, name=''):
        """Recursively flatten a nested dictionary or list."""
        if isinstance(x, dict):
            for a in x:
                flatten(x[a], name + a + '_')
        elif isinstance(x, list):
            for i, a in enumerate(x):
                flatten(a, name + str(i) + '_')
        else:
            out[name[:-1]] = x

    flatten(y)
    return out

def read_json_file(file_path):
    """Read and parse a JSON file, handling file not found and JSON decode errors."""
    try:
        with open(file_path, 'r') as file:
            return json.load(file)
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file '{file_path}' was not found.")
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        print(f"Error: The file '{file_path}' contains invalid JSON.")

def write_json_file(file_path, data):
    """Write a dictionary to a JSON file, handling I/O errors."""
    try:
        with open(file_path, 'w') as file:
            json.dump(data, file, indent=4)
    except IOError:
        print(f"Error: Could not write to file '{file_path}'.")

def main():
    """Main function to read, flatten, and write JSON data."""
    input_file = 'user_data.json'
    output_file = 'flattened_data.json'

    data = read_json_file(input_file)
    if data is None:
        return

    flattened_data = flatten_json(data)
    write_json_file(output_file, flattened_data)

    print(f"Flattened data successfully written to '{output_file}'.")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a function `render_products` for a web application that displays a list of products in a tabular format on a webpage. Each product is represented as an object with attributes `name`, `price`, and `description`. Your function should generate an HTML string that represents these products in a table. The table should have headers "Name", "Price", and "Description". Use a simple template engine to construct the HTML string.

**Input:**
- A list of `Product` objects, where each `Product` object has the following attributes:
  - `name`: A string representing the product's name.
  - `price`: A float representing the product's price.
  - `description`: A string representing the product's description.

**Output:**
- A string containing the HTML representation of the products in a table format.

**Sample Input:**
```python
product_list = [
    Product("Laptop", 999.99, "A high-performance laptop suitable for gaming and work."),
    Product("Smartphone", 299.99, "A compact smartphone with a sleek design."),
    Product("Smart Watch", 199.99, "A stylish smartwatch with health monitoring features."),
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```html
<table border='1'><tr><th>Name</th><th>Price</th><th>Description</th></tr><tr><td>Laptop</td><td>999.99</td><td>A high-performance laptop suitable for gaming and work.</td></tr><tr><td>Smartphone</td><td>299.99</td><td>A compact smartphone with a sleek design.</td></tr><tr><td>Smart Watch</td><td>199.99</td><td>A stylish smartwatch with health monitoring features.</td></tr></table>
```

```python
class Product:
    def __init__(self, name, price, description):
        self.name = name
        self.price = price
        self.description = description

def render_products(products):
    """
    Generates an HTML string representing a table of products.

    Parameters:
    products (list of Product): A list of Product objects.

    Returns:
    str: An HTML string representing the products in a table format.
    """
    html = "<table border='1'><tr><th>Name</th><th>Price</th><th>Description</th></tr>"
    for product in products:
        html += f"<tr><td>{product.name}</td><td>{product.price}</td><td>{product.description}</td></tr>"
    html += "</table>"
    return html

# Example usage
product_list = [
    Product("Laptop", 999.99, "A high-performance laptop suitable for gaming and work."),
    Product("Smartphone", 299.99, "A compact smartphone with a sleek design."),
    Product("Smart Watch", 199.99, "A stylish smartwatch with health monitoring features."),
]

print(render_products(product_list))
```<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Hangman` for a simple game where the player attempts to guess a word by suggesting letters. The game randomly selects a word from a given list. The player has a limited number of attempts (6) to guess the word. The class should include the following methods:

- `__init__(self, word_list)`: Initializes the game with a random word from the provided list.
- `display_state(self)`: Displays the current state of the game, including the guessed letters and remaining attempts.
- `guess_letter(self, letter)`: Processes a letter guess and updates the game state accordingly. Returns `True` if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise `False`.
- `is_game_over(self)`: Returns `True` if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise `False`.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```
Word: _ _ _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 6
Guessed letters: 
Guess a letter: p
Correct guess!
Word: p _ _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 6
Guessed letters: p 
Guess a letter: y
Correct guess!
Word: p y _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 6
Guessed letters: p y 
Guess a letter: t
Incorrect guess.
Word: p y _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 5
Guessed letters: p t y 
Guess a letter: h
Incorrect guess.
Word: p y _ _ _ _ 
Attempts left: 4
Guessed letters: h p t y 
Guess a letter: o
Correct guess!
Word: p y t h o _ 
Attempts left: 4
Guessed letters: h o p t y 
Guess a letter: n
Correct guess!
Congratulations! You've won!
```

```python
import random

class Hangman:
    def __init__(self, word_list):
        """
        Initializes the game with a random word from the provided list.
        """
        self.word = random.choice(word_list).lower()
        self.guessed_letters = set()
        self.remaining_attempts = 6
        self.current_state = ['_'] * len(self.word)
    
    def display_state(self):
        """
        Displays the current state of the game, including the guessed letters and remaining attempts.
        """
        print(f"Word: {' '.join(self.current_state)}")
        print(f"Attempts left: {self.remaining_attempts}")
        print(f"Guessed letters: {' '.join(sorted(self.guessed_letters))}")
    
    def guess_letter(self, letter):
        """
        Processes a letter guess and updates the game state accordingly.
        Returns True if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise False.
        """
        letter = letter.lower()
        if len(letter) != 1 or not letter.isalpha():
            print("Please enter a single valid letter.")
            return False
        if letter in self.guessed_letters:
            print("You have already guessed that letter.")
            return False
        
        self.guessed_letters.add(letter)
        
        if letter in self.word:
            for index, char in enumerate(self.word):
                if char == letter:
                    self.current_state[index] = letter
            print("Correct guess!")
            
            if '_' not in self.current_state:
                self.display_state()
                print("Congratulations! You've won!")
                return True
        else:
            self.remaining_attempts -= 1
            print("Incorrect guess.")
            
            if self.remaining_attempts == 0:
                self.display_state()
                print(f"Game over! The word was '{self.word}'.")
                return True
        return False
    
    def is_game_over(self):
        """
        Returns True if the game is over (either won or lost), otherwise False.
        """
        return self.remaining_attempts == 0 or '_' not in self.current_state

# Example usage
word_list = ['python', 'hangman', 'challenge', 'developer']
game = Hangman(word_list)
game.display_state()

while not game.is_game_over():
    guess = input("Guess a letter: ")
    game.guess_letter(guess)
    game.display_state()
```<#sep#>Implement a `BankAccount` class in Python that simulates basic bank account operations. The class should support the following functionalities:
- Initialize the account with an optional initial balance. If the initial balance is negative, raise a `ValueError`.
- Deposit a specified amount into the account. If the deposit amount is non-positive, raise a `ValueError`.
- Withdraw a specified amount from the account. If the withdrawal amount is non-positive or exceeds the current balance, raise a `ValueError`.
- Retrieve the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: `BankAccount(100).deposit(50).withdraw(30).get_balance()`
- Output: `120`
- Input: `BankAccount(100).withdraw(200)`
- Output: `ValueError: Insufficient funds for withdrawal.`
- Input: `BankAccount(100).deposit(-10)`
- Output: `ValueError: Deposit amount must be positive.`

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        """
        Initialize the bank account with an optional initial balance.
        Raises ValueError if the initial balance is negative.
        """
        if initial_balance < 0:
            raise ValueError("Initial balance cannot be negative.")
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.
        Raises ValueError if the deposit amount is non-positive.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount
        return self.balance

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.
        Raises ValueError if the withdrawal amount is non-positive or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount
        return self.balance

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Retrieve the current balance of the account.
        """
        return self.balance

# Example usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Creating a bank account with an initial balance of $100
    account = BankAccount(100)

    # Depositing $50
    print(f"Balance after depositing $50: ${account.deposit(50)}")

    # Withdrawing $30
    print(f"Balance after withdrawing $30: ${account.withdraw(30)}")

    # Getting the current balance
    print(f"Current balance: ${account.get_balance()}")

    # Trying to withdraw $200 (should raise an error)
    try:
        print(account.withdraw(200))
    except ValueError as e:
        print(e)

    # Trying to deposit a negative amount (should raise an error)
    try:
        print(account.deposit(-10))
    except ValueError as e:
        print(e)
```<#sep#>You are given a string representing a mathematical expression consisting of integers and the operators `+`, `-`, `*`, and `/`. Your task is to evaluate the expression using the Shunting Yard algorithm to convert it to Reverse Polish Notation (RPN) and then evaluate the RPN expression. Assume that the expression is well-formed and only contains non-negative integers and the specified operators. The input expression will not contain any unary operators or spaces between numbers and operators.

**Input:**
- A single string `expression` (1 ≤ len(expression) ≤ 100) representing the mathematical expression.

**Output:**
- An integer representing the result of the evaluated expression.

**Sample Input:**
```
3+5*(10-4)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
33
```

```python
def shunting_yard(expression):
    """
    Convert an infix expression to Reverse Polish Notation (RPN) using the Shunting Yard algorithm.
    
    :param expression: A string representing the infix expression.
    :return: A list of tokens representing the RPN expression.
    """
    precedence = {'+': 1, '-': 1, '*': 2, '/': 2}
    output = []
    operators = []
    tokens = []

    i = 0
    while i < len(expression):
        if expression[i].isdigit():
            num = expression[i]
            while i + 1 < len(expression) and expression[i + 1].isdigit():
                i += 1
                num += expression[i]
            tokens.append(num)
        else:
            tokens.append(expression[i])
        i += 1

    for token in tokens:
        if token.isdigit():
            output.append(token)
        elif token in precedence:
            while (operators and operators[-1] != '(' and
                   precedence[operators[-1]] >= precedence[token]):
                output.append(operators.pop())
            operators.append(token)
        elif token == '(':
            operators.append(token)
        elif token == ')':
            top_token = operators.pop()
            while top_token != '(':
                output.append(top_token)
                top_token = operators.pop()

    while operators:
        output.append(operators.pop())
    return output


def evaluate_rpn(rpn):
    """
    Evaluate a Reverse Polish Notation (RPN) expression.
    
    :param rpn: A list of tokens representing the RPN expression.
    :return: An integer representing the result of the evaluated RPN expression.
    """
    stack = []
    for token in rpn:
        if token.isdigit():
            stack.append(int(token))
        else:
            b = stack.pop()
            a = stack.pop()
            if token == '+':
                stack.append(a + b)
            elif token == '-':
                stack.append(a - b)
            elif token == '*':
                stack.append(a * b)
            elif token == '/':
                stack.append(a // b)  # using integer division as per the problem statement

    return stack[0]


# Example usage
expression = "3+5*(10-4)"
rpn = shunting_yard(expression)
result = evaluate_rpn(rpn)
print(result)  # Output should be 33
```<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script to validate a JSON configuration file for a web server. The configuration file must contain the following keys: `host`, `port`, `max_clients`, `timeout`, and `use_ssl`. Your script should check for the presence of these keys and validate their values according to the following constraints:

- `host`: A non-empty string.
- `port`: An integer between 1 and 65535, inclusive.
- `max_clients`: A positive integer.
- `timeout`: A number (integer or float) between 0.1 and 3600, inclusive.

Your function should return a string indicating whether the configuration is valid or specifying the first encountered error.

{
    "host": "localhost",
    "port": 8080,
    "max_clients": 100,
    "timeout": 30.5,
    "use_ssl": false
}

Configuration is valid.

**Sample Input:**

{
    "host": "localhost",
    "port": 70000,
    "max_clients": 100,

```python
import json

def validate_config(config):
    """
    Validates the configuration dictionary against expected keys and value constraints.
    
    Parameters:
    config (dict): The configuration dictionary to validate.
    
    Returns:
    str: A message indicating whether the configuration is valid or the first encountered error.
    """
    expected_keys = {
        "host": lambda x: isinstance(x, str) and x,
        "port": lambda x: isinstance(x, int) and 1 <= x <= 65535,
        "max_clients": lambda x: isinstance(x, int) and x > 0,
        "timeout": lambda x: isinstance(x, (int, float)) and 0.1 <= x <= 3600,
        "use_ssl": lambda x: isinstance(x, bool)
    }
    
    missing_keys = set(expected_keys.keys()) - set(config.keys())
    if missing_keys:
        return f"Configuration is missing the following keys: {', '.join(missing_keys)}"
    
    for key, validator in expected_keys.items():
        if not validator(config[key]):
            return f"Invalid value for '{key}': {config[key]}"
    
    return "Configuration is valid."

def load_and_validate_config(config_path):
    """
    Loads and validates a JSON configuration file.
    
    Parameters:
    config_path (str): The path to the configuration file.
    
    Returns:
    str: A message indicating whether the configuration is valid or the first encountered error.
    """
    try:
        with open(config_path, 'r') as file:
            config = json.load(file)
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        return "Error decoding JSON from the configuration file."
    except FileNotFoundError:
        return "Configuration file not found."
    except Exception as e:
        return f"An error occurred: {e}"
    
    return validate_config(config)

# Example usage:
# config_path = 'path_to_your_config_file.json'
# print(load_and_validate_config(config_path))

class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, year_published, genre, isbn):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance with the given attributes.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param year_published: The year the book was published.
        :param genre: The genre of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.

        self._author = author
        self._year_published = year_published
        self._genre = genre
        self._isbn = isbn

    def get_title(self):
        """
        Returns the title of the book.

        :return: The title of the book.
        """
        return self._title

    def set_title(self, title):
        """
        Sets a new title for the book.

        :param title: The new title of the book.
        """
        self._title = title

        """
        Returns the author of the book.

        :return: The author of the book.
        """
        return self._author

    def set_author(self, author):
        """
        Sets a new author for the book.

        :param author: The new author of the book.
        """
        self._author = author

    def get_year_published(self):
        """
        Returns the year the book was published.

        """
        return self._year_published

        self._year_published = year_published

        """

        """
        return self._genre

        """

        """
        self._genre = genre

        """
        Returns the ISBN of the book.

        return self._isbn

```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a modified version of the classic game "Tic-Tac-Toe" called "Tic-Tac-Two". In this game, there are two winners: the player who first achieves three of their marks in a row (horizontal, vertical, or diagonal), and the player with the most marks on the board. Your goal is to write a function `play_tic_tac_two()` that simulates the game, alternating turns between two players. The function should return a string indicating the two winners.

**Input:**
- The function does not take any input parameters. It will prompt the user for input during the game.

**Output:**
- A string indicating the first winner (the player who gets three marks in a row first) and the second winner (the player with the most marks on the board).

**Sample Interaction:**
```
 |  | 
---------
 |  | 
---------
 |  | 
---------
Player X's turn.
Enter row (0, 1, or 2): 0
Enter column (0, 1, or 2): 0
X |  | 
---------
 |  |

Enter column (0, 1, or 2): 2
X |  | 
---------
 | X | 
---------
 |  | O
---------
First winner: Player X, Second winner: Player X with 3 marks.
```

```python
def print_board(board):
    """
    Prints the current state of the Tic-Tac-Toe board.
    
    :param board: A 3x3 list of lists representing the board.
    """
    for row in board:
        print(" | ".join(row))
        print("-" * 9)

def check_winner(board, mark):
    """
    Checks if the player with the given mark has won the game.
    
    :param board: A 3x3 list of lists representing the board.
    :param mark: The mark of the player to check ('X' or 'O').
    :return: True if the player has won, False otherwise.
    """
    for i in range(3):
        if all([cell == mark for cell in board[i]]):  # Check row
            return True
        if all([board[j][i] == mark for j in range(3)]):  # Check column
            return True
    # Check diagonals
    if all([board[i][i] == mark for i in range(3)]):
        return True
    if all([board[i][2 - i] == mark for i in range(3)]):
        return True
    return False

def play_tic_tac_two():
    """
    Simulates the Tic-Tac-Two game and returns the results of the game.
    
    :return: A string indicating the first and second winners.
    """
    board = [[" " for _ in range(3)] for _ in range(3)]
    current_player = "X"
    player_marks = {"X": 0, "O": 0}
    first_winner = None
    
    while player_marks["X"] + player_marks["O"] < 9:
        print_board(board)
        print(f"Player {current_player}'s turn.")
        
        while True:
            try:
                row = int(input("Enter row (0, 1, or 2): "))
                col = int(input("Enter column (0, 1, or 2): "))
                if 0 <= row <= 2 and 0 <= col <= 2 and board[row][col] == " ":
                    board[row][col] = current_player
                    player_marks[current_player] += 1
                    break
                else:
                    print("Invalid move. Try again.")
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid input. Please enter numbers between 0 and 2.")
        
        if check_winner(board, current_player) and first_winner is None:
            first_winner = current_player
        
        # Alternate players
        current_player = "O" if current_player == "X" else "X"
    
    # Determine the second winner (most marks)
    second_winner = max(player_marks, key=player_marks.get)
    
    return f"First winner: Player {first_winner}, Second winner: Player {second_winner} with {player_marks[second_winner]} marks."

    Employee ID: [employee_id]
    Department: [department]
    Salary: $[salary] per year
    ```
  - `set_salary(new_salary)`: A method that updates the employee's salary only if the new salary is greater than the current salary. If the new salary is not greater, it should print:

  - `company_policy`: A static method that prints the company's employee policy:

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, department, salary):
        """
        Initializes a new Employee instance with the given details.

        :param name: str, the name of the employee
        :param employee_id: int, the unique identifier of the employee
        :param department: str, the department where the employee works
        :param salary: float, the annual salary of the employee
        """
        self.__name = name
        self.__employee_id = employee_id
        self.__department = department
        self.__salary = salary

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the employee's details in a formatted string.
        """
        print(f"Employee Name: {self.__name}")
        print(f"Employee ID: {self.__employee_id}")
        print(f"Department: {self.__department}")
        print(f"Salary: ${self.__salary:,.2f} per year")

    def set_salary(self, new_salary):
        """
        Updates the employee's salary if the new salary is greater than the current salary.

        :param new_salary: float, the new salary to be set
        """
        if new_salary > self.__salary:
            self.__salary = new_salary
        else:
            print("The new salary must be greater than the current salary.")

    @staticmethod
    def company_policy():
        """
        Prints the company's employee policy.
        """
        print("Company Employee Policy:")
        print("Minimum salary is $30,000 per year.")
        print("The company does not tolerate any form of discrimination.")

- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `published_date`: A date representing when the book was published.
- `isbn`: A unique string representing the ISBN number of the book.
- `genre`: A foreign key linking to the `Genre` model.
- `available_copies`: An integer representing the number of copies available in the library.
- `description`: A text field providing a description of the book.

Your task is to write the Django model code for both `Genre` and `Book` models. Ensure that the `__str__` method is properly defined for both models.

**Sample Input:**
```python
Genre.objects.create(name="Science Fiction")
Book.objects.create(
    title="Dune",
    author="Frank Herbert",
    published_date="1965-08-01",
    isbn="9780441172719",
    genre=Genre.objects.get(name="Science Fiction"),
    available_copies=5,
    description="A monumental science fiction novel."
)

Science Fiction

```python
from django.db import models

class Genre(models.Model):
    """
    Model representing a book genre.
    
    Fields:
    - name: A unique string representing the name of the genre.
    """
    name = models.CharField(max_length=100, unique=True)

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns the name of the genre.
        """
        return self.name

class Book(models.Model):
    """
    Model representing a book in the library.
    
    Fields:
    - title: A string representing the title of the book.
    - author: A string representing the author of the book.
    - published_date: A date representing when the book was published.
    - isbn: A unique string representing the ISBN number of the book.
    - genre: A foreign key linking to the Genre model.
    - available_copies: An integer representing the number of copies available in the library.
    - description: A text field providing a description of the book.
    """
    title = models.CharField(max_length=200)
    author = models.CharField(max_length=100)
    published_date = models.DateField()
    isbn = models.CharField(max_length=13, unique=True)
    genre = models.ForeignKey(Genre, on_delete=models.CASCADE)
    available_copies = models.IntegerField(default=0)
    description = models.TextField()

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns the title of the book.
        """
        return self.title
```<#sep#>You are given a CSV file with a header row. Your task is to write a Python script that calculates the average of a specified column. The column names in the CSV file are case-insensitive. The script should accept two command-line arguments: the path to the CSV file and the name of the column for which the average should be calculated. If the specified column does not exist or contains no numeric data, the script should output an appropriate error message and terminate.

**Input:**
- The first argument is the path to the CSV file.
- The second argument is the name of the column.

**Output:**
- If successful, print the average of the specified column in the format: `Average <Column_Name>: <Average_Value>`.
- If the column does not exist, print: `Error: Column '<Column_Name>' does not exist in the CSV file.`
- If the file is not found, print: `Error: File '<File_Name>' not found.`
- If no numeric data is found in the column, print: `Error: No numeric data found in column '<Column_Name>'.`

**Sample Input:**
```
python script.py data.csv "Age"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Average Age: 30.5
```

```python
import sys
import csv

def calculate_average_in_column(csv_file_path, column_name):
    """
    Calculate the average of a specified column in a CSV file.
    
    Args:
    csv_file_path (str): The path to the CSV file.
    column_name (str): The name of the column to calculate the average for.
    
    Returns:
    None
    """
    try:
        with open(csv_file_path, mode='r') as file:
            reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            column_name = column_name.lower()
            total = 0
            count = 0

            if column_name not in [name.lower() for name in reader.fieldnames]:
                print(f"Error: Column '{column_name}' does not exist in the CSV file.")
                sys.exit(1)

            for row in reader:
                try:
                    value = float(row[column_name])
                    total += value
                    count += 1
                except ValueError:
                    continue

            if count == 0:
                print(f"Error: No numeric data found in column '{column_name}'.")
                sys.exit(1)

            average = total / count
            print(f"Average {column_name.capitalize()}: {average}")
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: File '{csv_file_path}' not found.")
        sys.exit(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    if len(sys.argv) != 3:
        print("Usage: python script.py <csv_file_path> <column_name>")
        sys.exit(1)

    csv_file_path = sys.argv[1]
    column_name = sys.argv[2]
    calculate_average_in_column(csv_file_path, column_name)
```<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The class should encapsulate the following attributes: `title`, `author`, `isbn`, `available_copies`, and `borrow_date`. The `borrow_date` attribute should be initialized to `None` and updated to the current date when a book is borrowed. Implement methods to borrow and return books.

**Input:**
- The `Book` class will be instantiated with the following parameters:
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.
  - `available_copies`: An integer representing the number of copies available in the library.

**Output:**
- The `borrow_book` method should return a string indicating the success of the borrowing operation, including the borrow date and the number of copies left.
- The `return_book` method should return a string indicating the success of the return operation, including the number of copies now available.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Instantiate a `Book` object with the title "1984", author "George Orwell", ISBN "1234567890", and 3 available copies.
- Call `borrow_book()`: Output might be "Book '1984' borrowed on 2023-10-05. 2 copies left."
- Call `return_book()`: Output might be "Book '1984' returned. 3 copies now available."

```python
from datetime import date

class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, available_copies):
        """
        Initialize a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        :param available_copies: The number of available copies in the library.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.available_copies = available_copies
        self.borrow_date = None

    def borrow_book(self):
        """
        Borrow a copy of the book if available.

        :return: A message indicating the result of the borrowing operation.
        """
        if self.available_copies > 0:
            self.available_copies -= 1
            self.borrow_date = date.today()
            return f"Book '{self.title}' borrowed on {self.borrow_date}. {self.available_copies} copies left."
        else:
            return "No copies available to borrow."

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Return a copy of the book.

        :return: A message indicating the result of the return operation.
        """
        self.available_copies += 1
        self.borrow_date = None
        return f"Book '{self.title}' returned. {self.available_copies} copies now available."
